Improved Local Well-Posedness in Sobolev Spaces for Two-Dimensional Compressible Euler Equations
Huali Zhang

Abstract
. We establish the local existence and uniqueness of solutions to the two-dimensional compressible Euler equations with initial velocity 𝐯 0 \mathbf{v}_{0} , logarithmic density ρ 0 \rho_{0} , and specific vorticity w 0 w_{0} , which satisfy ( 𝐯 0 , ρ 0 , w 0 , ∇ w 0 ) ∈ H 7 4 + ​ ( ℝ 2 ) × H 7 4 + ​ ( ℝ 2 ) × H 3 2 ​ ( ℝ 2 ) × L 8 ​ ( ℝ 2 ) (\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) . The proof applies Smith-Tataru method [ 49 ] and the inherent wave-transport structure of the two-dimensional compressible Euler equations. The key observation is that Strichartz estimates hold when the regularity requirement for vorticity is lower than that for velocity and density, even though the gradient of vorticity appears as a source term in the velocity wave equation. Furthermore, our result presents an improvement of 1 4 \frac{1}{4} -order regularity compared to previous results [ 66 ] and [ 67 ] .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 76N10, 35R05, 35L60

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

In this paper, we study the two-dimensional compressible Euler equations, which are of the form

\begin{cases}\varrho_{t}+\text{div}\left(\varrho\mathbf{v}\right)=0,\quad t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\mathbf{v}_{t}+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathbf{v}+\frac{1}{\varrho}\nabla p(\varrho)=0,\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{v}=(v^{1},v^{2})^{\mathrm{T}},\varrho
, and
p
denote the fluid velocity, density, and pressure, respectively. Throughout the paper, we assume it’s far away from vacuum. If we denote the logarithmic density
\rho
by

\rho=\log\varrho,

(1.1)

the compressible Euler equations can be expressed as

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)\rho=-\textrm{div}\mathbf{v},\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\mathbf{v}_{t}+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathbf{v}+p^{\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho})\nabla\rho=0.\end{cases}

(1.2)

We consider the state function

p(\varrho)=\varrho^{\gamma}(\textrm{constant}\ \gamma\geq 1),

(1.3)

and the initial data as

(\mathbf{v},\rho)|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0}).

(1.4)

The compressible Euler equations (
1.2
) describes the motion of compressible ideal fluids, which has wide applications in gas dynamics and astrophysics. In mathematics, a fundamental question concerns the sharp local well-posedness in Sobolev spaces for the equations (
1.2
)-(
1.4
). Although a few results
[
7
,
17
,
41
,
62
,
66
,
67
]
address this question, it remains an open and challenging problem in both two and three dimensions. In this paper, we further investigate the well-posedness of rough solutions to the equations (
1.2
) in two dimensions, under lower regularity conditions than those established in previous studies
[
66
,
67
]
. To better understand the equations (
1.2
), we first review some historical results related to two specific cases: the incompressible case and the irrotational case.

For the Cauchy problem of
n
-D incompressible Euler equations:

\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}_{t}+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathbf{v}+\nabla p=0,\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}=0,\end{cases}

(1.5)

Kato and Ponce
[
26
]
proved the local existence and uniqueness of solutions to the equations (
1.5
) for initial velocity
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
s>1+\frac{n}{p},1<p<\infty
. Chae
[
13
,
14
]
extended the result of Kato and Ponce to the critical Triebel-Lizorkin space. For a discussion on the continuous dependence, we refer to the work of Tao
[
54
]
and Guo-Li
[
19
]
. In contrast, Bourgain and Li
[
11
,
12
]
proved the ill-posedness of (
1.5
) by constructing initial data
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in W^{1+\frac{n}{p},p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(for
n=2,3
) that leads to instantaneous blow-up. Recently, Kim and Jeong
[
28
]
proved the ill-posedness of (
1.5
) when
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in H^{\frac{n}{2}+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
n\geq 3
.

In the case of irrotational flow, the compressible Euler equations can be written as a specific quasilinear wave equation. More generally, quasilinear wave equations are expressed in the following form:

\begin{cases}&\square_{h(\phi)}\phi=q(d\phi,d\phi),\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t=0}=(\phi_{0},\phi_{1}),\end{cases}

(1.6)

where
\phi
is a scalar function,
d=(\partial_{t},\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\cdots,\partial_{n})
, and
h(\phi)
a Lorentzian metric depending on
\phi
, and
q
a quadratic term of
d\phi
. Set
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The local existence and uniqueness of (
1.6
) was established by Hughes-Kato-Marsden
[
22
]
when
s>{n}/{2}+1
. For the continuous dependence, one can refer Ifrim and Tataru’s paper
[
24
]
. On the other hand, Lindblad
[
36
]
established the ill-posedness of (
1.6
) by constructing counterexamples when
s=2,n=3
. Based on the idea of
[
36
]
, Ohlman
[
46
]
also proved the ill-posedness in two dimensions for
s=\frac{7}{4}
. A gap exists between the well-posedness results in
[
22
]
and the ill-posedness demonstrated in
[
36
]
. To bridge this regularity gap and relax the requirements on the initial data, a natural idea is to establish Strichartz-type estimates for the derivatives
d\phi
. As a first step, consider the linear wave equation with variable coefficients

\square_{h(t,x)}\phi=0,

(1.7)

as (
1.7
) provides a framework for understanding the nonlinear problems. Kapitanskij
[
29
]
and Mockenhaupt-Seeger-Sogge
[
44
]
discussed the Strichartz estimates for (
1.7
) with smooth coefficients
h
. For rough coefficients
h\in C^{2}
, the study of Strichartz estimates for (
1.7
) in two or three dimensions began with Smith’s result
[
51
]
. In all dimensions for
h\in C^{2}
, the corresponding Strichartz estimates was proved by Tataru
[
56
]
. Conversely, Smith and Sogge
[
52
]
constructed counterexamples showing that for
\alpha<2
there exists
h\in C^{\alpha}
for which the Strichartz estimates do not hold.

Secondly, the above regularity thresholds for (
1.6
) were independently improved by Bahouri-Chemin
[
9
]
and Tataru
[
55
]
for
s>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{7}{8},n=2
or
s>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{3}{4},n\geq 3
. Subsequently, Tataru
[
57
]
refined the regularity requirements to
s>\frac{n+1}{2}+\frac{1}{6},n\geq 3
. Meanwhile, Smith and Tataru
[
50
]
showed that the loss of one-sixth order derivatives is sharp for general variable coefficients
h
. Therefore, to further improve the results for (
1.6
), a new approach is necessary. Klainerman and Rodnianski
[
30
]
introduced a novel vector-field method and a remarkable Ricci curvature decomposition, establishing local existence and uniqueness for (
1.6
) when
s>2+\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{2}
and
n=3
. Based on
[
30
]
, Geba
[
25
]
successfully adapted the vector-field approach to the case
n=2
, where the regularity exponent satisfies
s>\frac{7}{4}+\frac{5-\sqrt{22}}{4}
. By representing solutions via wave packets, the sharp results in dimensions two and three were finally established by Smith and Tataru
[
49
]
(see also in Lindblad
[
36
]
and Ohlman
[
46
]
), where the regularity requires
s>\frac{7}{4},n=2
or
s>2,n=3
or
s>\frac{n+1}{2},4\leq n\leq 5
. An alternative proof of the 3D case was also obtained by Wang
[
61
]
using the vector-field approach. It is also very interesting to consider axisymmetric initial data; for this, please refer to the work by C.B. Wang
[
59
]
, Zha-Hidano
[
65
]
, Zhou-Lei
[
68
]
. Additionally, we should mention the significant progress made on low regularity solutions of the Einstein vacuum equations and membrane equations. We refer readers to the following papers: Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
2
]
, Allen-Andersson-Restuccia
[
3
]
, Andersson and Moncrief
[
6
]
, Ettinger and Lindblad
[
18
]
, Klainerman and Rodnianski
[
31
]
, Klainerman-Rodnianski-Szefel
[
32
]
, Moschidis-Rodnianski
[
45
]
, Tataru
[
58
]
, Wang
[
60
]
, Wang-Zhou
[
63
]
and among others.

In the general case, concerning to Cauchy problem of
n
-D compressible Euler equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
), it’s well-posed if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),s>1+\frac{n}{2}
and the density is far away from vacuum, please refer to Majda’s book
[
41
]
. Based on the pioneering work of Luk and Speck
[
37
,
38
]
, significant progress has been made in low regularity well-posedness for (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) via Strichartz estimates. In the case of 3D, Disconzi-Luo-Mazzone-Speck
[
17
]
proved the existence and uniqueness of solutions with initial entropy
S_{0}
, velocity
\mathbf{v}_{0}
, logarithmic density
\rho_{0}
and specific vorticity
\mathbf{w}_{0}
satisfying
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},S_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{3+}
and
\Delta S_{0},\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}_{0}\in C^{0,\delta}
, where
\delta
is a small positive number. Independently, Wang
[
62
]
also established the existence and uniqueness of solutions if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ 2<s^{\prime}<s
. The works
[
17
]
and
[
62
]
are based on vector-field approach. Using a different approach–Smith and Tataru’s method
[
49
]
, and combining with semiclassical analysis, Andersson and Zhang
[
7
]
were able to relax the regularity requirements in
[
17
,
62
]
, and proved the complete well-posedness if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},S_{0})\in H^{\frac{5}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{\frac{5}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{\frac{5}{2}+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In the case of 2D, Zhang
[
66
,
67
]
also proved the well-posedness of (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) by setting
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. For a discussion on ill-posedness or shock waves, we refer readers to important results due to Abbrescia-Speck
[
1
]
, An-Chen-Yin
[
4
,
5
]
, Christodoulou-Miao
[
15
]
, Huang-Kuang-Wang-Xiang
[
21
]
, Lei-Du-Zhang
[
33
]
, Luo-Yu
[
39
,
40
]
, Merle-Raphaël-Rodnianski
[
42
]
, Qu-Xin
[
47
]
, Sideris
[
48
]
, Speck
[
53
]
, and Yin
[
64
]
. Additionally, we also mention several remarkable results on free-boundary problems of the compressible Euler equations, particularly those by Avadanei
[
8
]
, Coutand-Lindblad-Shkoller
[
16
]
, Jang-Masmoudi
[
20
]
, and Ifrim-Tataru
[
23
]
.

1.2.
Motivation

As established in
[
66
,
67
]
, the regularity conditions for velocity and density are weaker than those presented in Majda’s book
[
41
]
, due to the Strichartz estimates. Furthermore, the regularity conditions imposed on velocity and density align with those for quasilinear wave equations
[
49
]
. The current result
[
7
]
shows that 3D compressible Euler equation is well-posed if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. However, the current 2D results
[
66
,
67
]
require that
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),w_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
or
w_{0}\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Compared with the above results in 2D and 3D, the regularity requirements for vorticity in 2D results
[
66
,
67
]
remain less desirable. This motivates our investigation into two-dimensional vorticity-specific regularity conditions, with the aim of establishing well-posedness at the same level in 3D. Indeed, due to the finite propagation speed, we can consider the problem as a perturbation of a flat Minkowski metric. Let us start with the standard linear wave equation:

\begin{cases}&\square f=0,\quad t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=0}=(f_{0},0),\end{cases}

where
\square=\partial_{t}^{2}-(\partial^{2}_{1}+\partial^{2}_{2})
. By applying Keel-Tao’s result
[
27
]
and Knapp’s counterexample, the sharp Strichartz estimates hold:

\|df\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{7}{4}+}}.

Therefore, for the following wave-transport system (see Lemma
1.1
below for details)

\displaystyle\square_{g}\mathbf{v}\approx

\displaystyle\nabla w+(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\cdot(d\mathbf{v},d\rho),

(1.8)

\displaystyle\square_{g}\rho\approx

\displaystyle(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\cdot(d\mathbf{v},d\rho),

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}w=

\displaystyle 0,

(1.10)

we can expect the Strichartz estimates
\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}
by setting
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}
. For
\nabla w
appears as a source term in (
1.8
), a general idea suggests the initial vorticity
w_{0}\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}
. This is the result in
[
66
,
67
]
. By careful analysis, we can find that the setting,
w_{0}\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}
is much higher than desired. Let us make a short explanation as follows.

We introduce a decomposition for the 2D velocity by
1
1

1

This is inspired by Wang’s 3D result
[
62
]
.

v^{i}=v^{i}_{-}+v^{i}_{+},\quad v^{i}_{-}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\left(-\epsilon^{ia}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{a}w\right),\quad i=1,2.

Then
\mathbf{v}_{+}=(v_{+}^{1},v_{+}^{2})
and
\mathbf{v}_{-}=(v_{-}^{1},v_{-}^{2})
satisfy

\displaystyle\square_{g}\mathbf{v}_{+}\approx

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{v}_{-}+(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\cdot(d\mathbf{v},d\rho),

(1.11)

\displaystyle\Delta\mathbf{T}\mathbf{v}_{-}\approx

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla w.

(1.12)

If
g
is a flat metric, we can directly obtain

\begin{split}\|d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim&\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s}_{x}}+\|(\mathbf{v},\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{\frac{7}{4}+}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{7}{4}+}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{7}{4}+}}+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{1+}}.\end{split}

The above inequality, combining with
\|d\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{1+}}
, imply that

\begin{split}\|d\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim&\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{7}{4}+}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{7}{4}+}}+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{1+}}\\
\lesssim&\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{7}{4}+}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{7}{4}+}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{1+}}.\end{split}

When the metric
g
in (
1.11
) is non-flat and depends on
\mathbf{v}
and
\rho
, to obtain Strichartz estimates, we need to study the geometric properties of null hypersurfaces. This is controlled by
\square_{g}g
. If we set
w\in H^{s^{\prime}}_{x}
(
s^{\prime}\leq\frac{7}{4}
), we have

\square_{g}g=\nabla w+(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\cdot(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\in H_{x}^{s^{\prime}-1}.

Definitely, it’s crucial for us to find a condition for
s^{\prime}
to establish the Strichartz estimates. Through a careful adaptation of Smith-Tataru’s method
[
49
]
, we find that the Sobolev regularity indices
s^{\prime}>\frac{3}{2}
is sufficient. Therefore, applying equations (
1.9
) and (
1.11
), we can obtain a bound for Strichartz estimates
\|d\mathbf{v}_{+},d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}
. As for
d\mathbf{v}_{-}
, using (
1.12
), we also get a bound using Sobolev’s imbeddings. As a result, we can derive the desired Strichartz estimates
\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}
if
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{\frac{7}{4}+}\times L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{\frac{7}{4}+}\times L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}
. This implies that we should assume
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+}
. Meanwhile, we also need to close the energy estimates with
w\in L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}
for the vorticity. Therefore, using (
1.10
), we have

\mathbf{T}\nabla w=\nabla\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla w.

Due to commutator and product estimates, the additional condition
\nabla w\in L_{x}^{8}
is necessary.

In a word, we first prove the local existence and uniqueness of solutions for the two dimensional compressible Euler equations with initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
. We also establish two additional useful properties:
(i)
a Strichartz estimate for linear wave equations endowed with an acoustic metric, and
(ii)
a type of Strichartz estimate for solutions with a regularity of velocity
\frac{7}{4}+
, density
\frac{7}{4}+
, and vorticity
1+
. Secondly, if the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})
belong to the spaces
H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, by applying frequency truncation on the initial data, a stronger Strichartz estimate can be established on a short time interval. Moreover, this allows us to obtain a sequence of solutions defined on these short intervals. After that, we extend this sequence of solutions from short time intervals to a fixed, regular time interval, and a Strichartz estimate also holds with a loss of derivatives
2
2

2

This argument is inspired by the work of Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
2
]
, which has been developed by Andersson and Zhang
[
7
]
in 3D compressible Euler equations.
. Based on this Strichartz estimates, we prove the local existence and uniqueness of solutions to the 2D compressible Euler equations for initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Compared to the earlier work
[
66
,
67
]
, our result reduces the required regularity for vorticity by one-quarter order.

Next, we give some necessary notations in this paper.

1.3.
Notations

•

We give the notations
d=(\partial_{t},\partial_{1},\partial_{2})^{\mathrm{T}}
and
\partial_{0}=\partial_{t}
.

•

We set
\left<\xi\right>=(1+|\xi|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and denote by
\left<\nabla\right>
the corresponding Bessel potential multiplier.

•

We denote the fractional Laplacian operator

\Lambda_{x}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\Delta=\partial^{2}_{1}+\partial^{2}_{2}.

•

Let
\zeta
be a smooth function with support in the shell
\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\frac{1}{2}\leq|\xi|\leq 2\}
. Here,
\xi
denotes the variable of the spatial Fourier transform. Let
\zeta
also satisfy the
condition
\sum_{\lambda=2^{k},k\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta(\lambda\xi)=1
.

•

Following the book
[
10
]
, we introduce the Littlewood-Palay operator
P_{\lambda}
with the frequency
\lambda=2^{k}(k\in\mathbb{Z})
, which satisfies

P_{\lambda}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi}\zeta(\lambda^{-1}\xi)\hat{f}(\xi)d\xi.

•

We also set

f_{<\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda}P_{\lambda}f.

•

When the function
f
is related to space variables, we use the notation
\|f\|_{H^{s}}=\|f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
. When
f
is related to both time and space variables, we use the notation
\|f\|_{H_{x}^{s}}=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.
Similarly, we use notation
\|f\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
if
f
depends only the spatial variables, and use
\|f\|_{L_{x}^{p}}=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
if
f
depends on both time and space variables. For mixed norms shall be denoted by
\|f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}
.

•

We use four small parameters

\epsilon_{3}\ll\epsilon_{2}\ll\epsilon_{1}\ll\epsilon_{0}\ll 1,

(1.13)

and three notations

\delta\in(0,s-\frac{7}{4}),\quad\delta_{0}\in(0,s_{0}-\frac{7}{4}),\quad\delta_{1}=\frac{s-\frac{7}{4}}{10}.

•

Assume there holds

|\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0}|\leq C_{0},\qquad c_{s}|_{t=0}>c_{0}>0,

(1.14)

where
C_{0}
and
c_{0}
are positive constants.

•

In the following, constant
C
depending only on
C_{0},c_{0}
shall be called universal. Unless otherwise stated, all constants that appear are universal in this sense. The notation
X\lesssim Y
means
X\leq CY
, where
C
is a universal constant, possibly depending on
C_{0},c_{0}
. Similarly, we write
X\simeq Y
when
C_{1}Y\leq X\leq C_{2}Y
, with
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
universal constants, and
X\ll Y
when
X\leq CY
for a sufficiently large constant
C
.

1.4.
Statement of the result

Before stating our result, let us introduce a definition of the sound speed, logarithmic density, specific vorticity, and acoustic metric.

Definition 1.1
.

(
[
37
]
, Definition 2.1)
We denote the speed of sound

c_{s}=\sqrt{\frac{dp}{d\varrho}}.

(1.15)

In view of (
1.3
) and (
1.15
), we have

c_{s}=c_{s}(\varrho)

(1.16)

and

c^{\prime}_{s}=\frac{\text{d}c_{s}}{\text{d}\varrho}.

(1.17)

Remark 1.1
.

Due to (
1.16
), (
1.17
), and (
1.1
), thus
c_{s}
and
c^{\prime}_{s}
are also functions depending on
\rho
.

Definition 1.2
.

(
[
37
]
, Definition 2.1)
We denote the specific vorticity
w
as

w=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}{{\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}}}.

(1.18)

Definition 1.3
.

(
[
37
]
, Definition 2.3)
We define the acoustical metric
g
and the inverse acoustical metric
g^{-1}
relative to the Cartesian coordinates as follows:

\begin{split}&g=-dt\otimes dt+c_{s}^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(dx^{i}-v^{i}dt\right)\otimes\left(dx^{i}-v^{i}dt\right),\\
&g^{-1}=-(\partial_{t}+v^{i}\partial_{i})\otimes(\partial_{t}+v^{j}\partial_{j})+c_{s}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\otimes\partial_{i}.\end{split}

(1.19)

Definition 1.4
.

Define a decomposition for the velocity
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{+}+\mathbf{v}_{-}
and

v^{i}=v_{+}^{i}+v_{-}^{i}.

(1.20)

Above, the vector
\mathbf{v}_{-}=(v_{-}^{1},v_{-}^{2})^{\mathrm{T}}
is defined by

-\Delta v_{-}^{i}=\epsilon^{ia}\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}\partial_{a}w.

(1.21)

Based on these definitions, let us introduce the equivalent system of (
1.2
) under new variables.

Lemma 1.1
.

(
[
37
]
, Proposition 2.4)
The compressible Euler equations (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) also satisfies the following wave-transport system

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}v^{i}=-\epsilon^{ia}e^{\rho}c^{2}_{s}\partial_{a}w+Q^{i},\\
&\square_{g}\rho=\mathcal{D},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
&\mathbf{T}w=0.\end{cases}

(1.22)

Above,
Q^{i}
(
i=1,2
),
\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathbf{T}
are defined by

\begin{split}&Q^{i}=2\epsilon^{ia}c^{2}_{s}w\partial_{a}\rho-\left(1+c_{s}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{s}\right)g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}v^{i},\\
&\mathcal{D}=-3c_{s}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}\rho+2\textstyle{\sum}_{1\leq a<b\leq 2}\big\{\partial_{a}v^{a}\partial_{b}v^{b}-\partial_{a}v^{b}\partial_{b}v^{a}\big\},\\
&\mathbf{T}=\partial_{t}+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla,\end{split}

(1.23)

and

\epsilon^{ia}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if \ $i=a$ },\\
1,&\text{ if \ $i<a$ },\\
-1,&\text{ if \ $i>a$ }.\end{cases}

We also define
\mathbf{Q}=(Q^{1},Q^{2})^{\mathrm{T}}
.

We next introduce the first theorem in the paper.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
and (
1.14
) hold. Let
M_{0}
be any positive constant. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.22
). If

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|{\rho}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq M_{0},

there exists two positive constants
T
(depending on
C_{0},c_{0},M_{0},s,s_{0}
) such that the Cauchy problem (
1.22
) is locally well-posed. Precisely,

(1)

there exists a unique solution
(\mathbf{v},{\rho})\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{s})\cap C^{1}([0,T],H_{x}^{s-1})
,
w\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}})

\cap C^{1}([0,T],H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{5}{4}})
,
\nabla w\in C([0,T],L_{x}^{8})
, and
(d\mathbf{v},d{{\rho}})\in L^{4}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}
,

(2)

for any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[0,T]
, the following linear equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B,\qquad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
&f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r},\\
&\partial_{t}f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{1}\in H_{x}^{r-1},\end{cases}

(1.24)

admits a solution
f\in C([0,T],H^{r})\times C^{1}([0,T],H^{r-1})
and the following estimates hold:

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}).

(1.25)

Additionally, the following estimates hold, provided
a<r-\frac{3}{4}
,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(1.26)

and the same estimates hold with
\left<\nabla\right>^{a}
replaced by
\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d
. Here
C_{M_{0}}
is a positive constant depending on
C_{0},c_{0},s,s_{0}
and
M_{0}
.

Remark 1.2
.

(1)

The initial condition
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}
ensures the Strichartz estimate for 2D wave system. The vorticity regularity
w_{0}\in H^{\frac{3}{2}+}
controls the geometry condition of null hypersurface in section
4
. Meanwhile, the additional condition
\nabla w_{0}\in L^{8}
is imposed specifically to close the energy bounds for vorticity due to product estimates.

(2)

For the free boundary problems, there is no Strichartz estimates of solutions. Therefore, on the study of low regularity well-posedness, the free boundary problems is much different from Cauchy problem. For more details, please refer to Avadanei
[
8
]
and Ifrim-Tataru
[
23
]
.

Let us now state our second theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume
s\in(\frac{7}{4},2]
and (
1.14
) hold. Consider the problem (
1.22
). Let
M_{0}
be any positive constant. If

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|{\rho}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq M_{0},

(1.27)

then there exists two positive constants
T^{*}
(depending on
C_{0},c_{0},M_{0},s
) such that the Cauchy problem (
1.22
) is locally well-posed. Precisely,

(1)

there exists a unique solution
(\mathbf{v},{\rho})\in C([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{s})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{s-1})
,
w\in C([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}})

\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
\nabla w\in C([0,T^{*}],L_{x}^{8})
, and
(d\mathbf{v},d{{\rho}})\in L^{4}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}
,

(2)

for any
s-\frac{3}{4}\leq r\leq\frac{11}{4}
, and for each
t_{0}\in[0,T^{*}]
, the following linear equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B,\qquad(t,x)\in[0,T^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
&f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r},\\
&\partial_{t}f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{1}\in H_{x}^{r-1},\end{cases}

(1.28)

admits a solution
f\in C([0,T^{*}],H^{r})\times C^{1}([0,T^{*}],H^{r-1})
and the following estimates hold:

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}).

Additionally, the following estimates hold, provided
a<r-(s-1)
,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}),

and the same estimates hold with
\left<\nabla\right>^{a}
replaced by
\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d
. Here
C_{M_{0}}
is a positive constant depending on
C_{0},c_{0},s
and
M_{0}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Compared with Theorem
1.2
, Theorem
1.3
lowers the regularity exponent of the vorticity from
\frac{3}{2}+
to exactly
\frac{3}{2}
. This reduction is non-trivial because, if we apply Smith-Tataru’s approach directly, we cannot obtain the Strichartz estimates for velocity and density when
w_{0}\in H^{\frac{3}{2}}
.

When
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we first use the frequency truncation to get a sequence of initial data belonging in
H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Based on Theorem
1.2
, a sequence of solutions on a short time-interval, and these intervals depends on the size of frequency.
By extending the solutions from these short time intervals to a uniformly regular time-interval, a Strichartz estimate with a loss of derivatives can be obtained. This leads us to prove the local existence and uniqueness of solutions to the 2D compressible Euler equations for initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Remark 1.4
.

(1)

Since the regularity needed for vorticity is less stringent than that for velocity and density, the theorems
1.2
and
1.3
can be seen as a non-trivial extension from 2D quasilinear wave equation to the 2D quasilinear wave-transport system. Moreover, the 2D results presented in Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
of this paper may be comparable to the known 3D results
[
7
,
62
]
.

(2)

Referring to the ill-posedness result in
[
4
]
, we expect that the regularity of velocity and density in Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
is optimal. Indeed, the sharp regularity of velocity, density, and vorticity for the Cauchy problem of 2D compressible Euler equations remains an open and challenging problem.

(3)

Once it’s far away from vacuum, Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
also hold for a more general state
p=p(\varrho)
such that
p^{\prime}(\varrho)>0
.

1.5.
Outline of the paper

The organization of the remainder of this paper is as follows. In Section 2, we prove the total energy estimates and uniqueness of solutions. In Section 3-7, we give a complete proof for Theorem
1.2
. In the subsequent Section
8
, we present a proof for Theorem
1.3
.

2.
Structures and energy estimates

In this part we aim to give good formulations of (
1.2
), energy estimates and uniqueness theorems.

2.1.
Good formulations

Let us start by introducing a hyperbolic system of compressible Euler equations (
1.2
).

Lemma 2.1
.

[
35
]

Let
\mathbf{v}
and
\rho
be a solution of (
1.2
). Set
\mathbf{U}=(v^{1},v^{2},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}))^{\mathrm{T}}
. Then (
1.2
) satisfies the following symmetric hyperbolic system

A^{0}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}+{A}^{1}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{1}\mathbf{U}+{A}^{2}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{2}\mathbf{U}=0,

(2.1)

where
A^{\alpha}(\alpha=0,1,2)
is defined by

A^{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}&0&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{\rho}&0\\
0&0&\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}c_{s}^{-2}\end{array}\right),\quad A^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v^{1}&0&1\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v^{1}&0\\
1&0&v^{1}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}c_{s}^{-2}\end{array}\right),

A^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v^{2}&0&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v^{2}&1\\
0&1&v^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}c_{s}^{-2}\end{array}\right).

Remark 2.1
.

We also mention that there is another symmetrization due to Lefloch and Ukai
[
34
]
.

Next, let us introduce a good wave equation for
\mathbf{v}_{+}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

(
[
67
]
, Lemma 2.3)
Let
\mathbf{v}
and
\rho
be a solution of (
1.2
) and
w
be defined in (
1.18
). Let
\mathbf{v}_{+}
and
\mathbf{v}_{-}
be denoted in Definition
1.4
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{+}=(v_{+}^{1},v_{+}^{2})^{\mathrm{T}}
satisfies

\begin{split}&\square_{g}v^{i}_{+}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}v_{-}^{i}+Q^{i}.\end{split}

(2.2)

Lemma 2.3
.

(
[
67
]
, Lemma 2.8)
Let
\mathbf{v}
and
\rho
be a solution of (
1.2
) and
w
be defined in (
1.18
). Then
\nabla w
satisfies

\mathbf{T}(\partial_{i}w)=\partial_{i}v^{j}\partial_{j}w,\quad i=1,2.

(2.3)

2.2.
Commutator and product estimates

We first introduce a classical commutator estimate.

Lemma 2.4
.

[
26
]

Let
a\geq 0
and
\Lambda_{x}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Then for any scalar functions
h
and
f
, we have

\|\Lambda_{x}^{a}(hf)-(\Lambda_{x}^{a}h)f\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|\Lambda_{x}^{a-1}h\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|h\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\Lambda_{x}^{a}f\|_{L^{q}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
.

Next, let us introduce some product estimates.

Lemma 2.5
.

[
26
]

Let
F(u)
be a smooth function of
u
,
F(0)=0
and
u\in L^{\infty}_{x}
. For any
s\geq 0
, we have

\|F(u)\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H_{x}^{s}}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}).

Lemma 2.6
.

[
49
]

Suppose that
0\leq r,r^{\prime}<\frac{n}{2}
and
r+r^{\prime}>\frac{n}{2}
. Then

\|hf\|_{H^{r+r^{\prime}-\frac{n}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{r,r^{\prime}}\|h\|_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Moreover, if
-r\leq r^{\prime}\leq r
and
r>\frac{n}{2}
, we have

\|hf\|_{H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{r,r^{\prime}}\|h\|_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Finally, let us introduce a modified Duhamel’s principle.

Lemma 2.7
.

(
[
67
]
, Lemma 7.3)
Let the metric
g
be defined in (
1.3
). If
f(t,x;\tau)
is the solution of

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=0,\quad t>\tau,\\
&(f,\mathbf{T}f)|_{t=\tau}=-(\Theta,B)(\tau,x),\end{cases}

then the function

V(t,x)=\int^{t}_{0}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau,

is the solution of the linear wave equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}V=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B,\\
&(V,\mathbf{T}V)|_{t=0}=(0,-\Theta(0,x)).\end{cases}

2.3.
Energy estimates

We first introduce some energy estimates for lower-order terms in (
1.22
).

Lemma 2.8
.

(
[
67
]
, Lemma 2.12 )
Assume
s\in(\frac{7}{4},2]
. Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho)
be a solution of (
1.2
) and
w
be defined in (
1.18
). Let
\mathbf{v}_{-}
be defined in (
1.21
). Let
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathbf{Q}
be stated in (
1.23
). Then the following estimates

\|\mathcal{D},\mathbf{Q}\|_{H_{x}^{s-1}}\lesssim\|d\rho,d\mathbf{v}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{H_{x}^{s}},

(2.4)

and

\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\|w\|_{H_{x}^{1+}},

(2.5)

hold. Moreover, the function
\mathbf{v}_{-}
satisfies

\|\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{H_{x}^{2+}}\lesssim(1+\|{\rho}\|_{H^{s}_{x}})\|w\|_{H_{x}^{1+}}.

(2.6)

Finally, we will present a total energy theorem in the following, which will be very useful.

Theorem 2.9
.

(Total energy estimate:type 1)
Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
. Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho)
be a solution of (
1.2
) and
w
be defined as in (
1.18
). Set

E(t)=\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|^{2}_{L^{8}_{x}},

and

E_{0}=\|\rho_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{8}}.

If
0<t\leq 1
, then the following energy inequality holds:

\begin{split}E(t)\leq&CE_{0}\exp\left\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau+CE_{0}\exp(\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau)\right\}.\end{split}

(2.7)

Proof.

By using (
2.1
), for any
t>0
, we have

\begin{split}&\|\rho\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}\leq C\left(\|\rho_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\right)+C{\int^{t}_{0}}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(\|\rho\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}})d\tau.\end{split}

(2.8)

Due to Gronwall’s inequality, it yields

\begin{split}&\|(\rho,\mathbf{v})\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}\leq C\|(\rho_{0},\mathbf{v}_{0})\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\exp\left(\int^{t}_{0}\|(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau\right).\end{split}

(2.9)

Multiplying with
w
on the third equation in (
1.22
) and integrating it on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we find

\begin{split}&\|w\|^{2}_{L_{x}^{2}}\leq C\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{L_{x}^{2}}+\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|w\|^{2}_{L_{x}^{2}}d\tau.\end{split}

(2.10)

Operating derivatives
\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}
on
\mathbf{T}w=0
, we then have

\begin{split}\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w)=[v^{i},\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}]\partial_{i}w.\end{split}

(2.11)

Multiplying with
\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w
on (
2.11
) and integrating it on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which implies that

\begin{split}\|\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq&\|\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}+C\left|\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\cdot|\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w|^{2}dxd\tau\right|\\
&+C\left|\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[v^{i},\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}]\partial_{i}w\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}wdxd\tau\right|\\
\leq&\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}d\tau\\
&+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\frac{8}{3}}_{x}}\|\nabla w\|_{L^{8}_{x}}\|\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}w\|_{L^{2}_{x}}d\tau.\end{split}

(2.12)

Combining (
2.10
) with (
2.12
) yields to

\begin{split}\|w\|^{2}_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}\leq&\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|w\|^{2}_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}d\tau\\
&+\int^{t}_{0}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|\nabla w\|_{L^{8}_{x}}\|w\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}d\tau.\end{split}

(2.13)

Applying (
2.3
), we also obtain the energy inequality

\begin{split}\|\nabla w\|^{2}_{L^{8}_{x}}\leq&\|\nabla w_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{8}_{x}}+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\nabla w\|^{2}_{L^{8}_{x}}d\tau.\end{split}

(2.14)

Thanks to (
2.8
), (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), we get

E(t)\leq CE_{0}+C\int^{t}_{0}\left(\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{v}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\right)E(\tau)d\tau.

By Gronwall’s inequality, we derive

E(t)\leq CE_{0}\exp\left\{\int^{t}_{0}\left(\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{v}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\right)d\tau\right\}.

(2.15)

Due to (
2.9
), then (
2.15
) becomes

\begin{split}E(t)\leq&CE_{0}\exp\left\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau+CE_{0}\int^{t}_{0}\exp\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau^{\prime}\}d\tau\right\}\\
\leq&CE_{0}\exp\left\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau+CtE_{0}\exp(\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau)\right\}.\end{split}

(2.16)

Applying (
2.16
) and
0<t\leq 1
, we finally have

\begin{split}E(t)\leq&CE_{0}\exp\left\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau+CE_{0}\exp(\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau)\right\}.\end{split}

This implies that (
2.7
) holds. Therefore, we complete the proof of this theorem.
∎

Theorem 2.10
.

(Total energy estimate:type 2)
Assume
s\in(\frac{7}{4},2]
. Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho)
be a solution of (
1.2
) and
w
be defined as in (
1.18
). Set

\bar{E}(t)=\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\|\nabla w\|^{2}_{L^{8}_{x}},

and

\bar{E}_{0}=\|\rho_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{8}}.

If
0<t\leq 1
, then the following energy inequality holds:

\begin{split}\bar{E}(t)\leq&C\bar{E}_{0}\exp\left\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau+C\bar{E}_{0}\exp(\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau)\right\}.\end{split}

(2.17)

Proof.

Taking derivatives
\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}
on
\mathbf{T}w=0
, we get

\begin{split}\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w)=[v^{i},\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}]\partial_{i}w.\end{split}

(2.18)

Multiplying (
2.18
) with
\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w
and integrating it on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which implies that

\begin{split}\|\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq&\|\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}+C\left|\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\cdot|\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w|^{2}dxd\tau\right|\\
&+C\left|\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[v^{i},\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}]\partial_{i}w\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}wdxd\tau\right|\\
\leq&\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}}+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}d\tau\\
&+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\frac{8}{3}}_{x}}\|\nabla w\|_{L^{8}_{x}}\|\Lambda_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|_{L^{2}_{x}}d\tau.\end{split}

(2.19)

Combining (
2.10
) with (
2.19
) yields to

\begin{split}\|w\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}_{x}}\leq&\|w_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}_{x}}+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|w\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}_{x}}d\tau\\
&+\int^{t}_{0}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|\nabla w\|_{L^{8}_{x}}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}_{x}}d\tau.\end{split}

(2.20)

Applying (
2.8
), (
2.14
) and (
2.20
), we obtain

\bar{E}(t)\leq C\bar{E}_{0}+C\int^{t}_{0}\left(\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{v}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\right)\bar{E}(\tau)d\tau.

By Gronwall’s inequality, then (
2.17
) holds. We thus complete the proof of theorem
2.10
.
∎

2.4.
Uniqueness of the solution

We now introduce two corollaries regarding the uniqueness of solutions, which can be obtained directly from Theorems
2.9
and
2.10
.

Corollary 2.11
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
and (
1.14
) hold. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.22
) with the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},

\nabla w_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}\times L^{8}
. If there exists a solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)
for (
1.22
) and

(\mathbf{v},\rho)\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{s})
,
w\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}})
,
\nabla w\in C([0,T],L^{8}_{x})
and
(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\in{L^{4}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
, then it’s unique.

Corollary 2.12
.

Assume
s\in(\frac{7}{4},2]
and (
1.14
) hold. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.22
) with the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},

\nabla w_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{\frac{3}{2}}\times L^{8}
. If there exists a solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)
for (
1.22
) and

(\mathbf{v},\rho)\in C([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{s})
,
w\in C([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}})
,
\nabla w\in C([0,T^{*}],L^{8}_{x})
and
(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\in{L^{4}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
, then it’s unique.

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Observing the conclusions in Theorem
1.2
, it includes existence, uniqueness of solutions, and Strichartz estimates. By using Corollary
2.11
, the uniqueness of solution also holds once we prove the existence of solutions and Strichartz estimates. Therefore, we only need to discuss the existence of solutions and Strichartz estimates. Our idea here is to reduce the proofs of Theorem
1.2
to the case of smooth, compactly supported and small initial data.

3.1.
A reduction to the case of smooth initial data

Proposition 3.1
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
,
\delta\in(0,s-\frac{7}{4})
, and (
1.14
) hold. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.22
). For each
M_{0}>0
, there exists positives
T
and
M_{1}
(depending on
s,s_{0},C_{0},c_{0},M_{0}
) such that, for each smooth initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0})
which satisfies

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq M_{0},\end{split}

there exists a smooth solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)
to (
1.22
) on
[-T,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L_{x}^{8}}\leq M_{1}.

(3.1)

Furthermore, the solution satisfies

(1)

dispersive estimate for
\mathbf{v}
,
\rho
and
\mathbf{v}_{+}

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x}}\leq M_{1}.

(3.2)

(2)

Let a function
f
satisfy equation (
1.24
). For each
1\leq r\leq s+1
, the Cauchy problem (
1.24
) is well-posed in
H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
, and the following estimates

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(3.3)

and

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}).

(3.4)

hold, where
a<r-\frac{3}{4}
and
C_{M_{0}}
is a constant depending on
s,s_{0},C_{0},c_{0},M_{0}
. The similar estimates hold if we replace
\left<\nabla\right>^{a}
by
\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d
.

Next, we will give a proof for Theorem
1.2
based on Proposition
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
by using Proposition
3.1
.

Consider the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}\times L^{8}
and

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|{\rho}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq M_{0}.

Let
\{(\mathbf{v}_{0k},\rho_{0k},w_{0k},\nabla w_{0k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
be a sequence of smooth data which converges to
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0},\nabla w_{0})
in
H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}\times L^{8}
, where
w_{0k}
is given by

w_{0k}=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{0k}}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}_{0k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

By use of Proposition
3.1
, for each data
(\mathbf{v}_{0k},\rho_{0k},w_{0k})
, there exists a solution
(\mathbf{v}_{k},\rho_{k},w_{k})
to (
1.22
), and corresponds to the initial data

(\mathbf{v}_{k},\rho_{k},w_{k})|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}_{0k},\rho_{0k},w_{0k}).

We note that the solution of (
1.22
) also satisfies the symmetric hyperbolic system (
2.1
). Let
\mathbf{U}_{k}=(\mathbf{v}_{k},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{k}}))^{\mathrm{T}},k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. For
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we get

\begin{split}&A^{0}(\mathbf{U}_{k})\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}_{k}+\sum^{2}_{i=1}A^{i}(\mathbf{U}_{k})\partial_{i}\mathbf{U}_{k}=0,\\
&A^{0}(\mathbf{U}_{l})\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}_{l}+\sum^{2}_{i=1}A^{i}(\mathbf{U}_{l})\partial_{i}\mathbf{U}_{l}=0.\end{split}

Applying the standard energy estimates to
\mathbf{U}_{k}-\mathbf{U}_{l}
, it yields

\frac{d}{dt}\|\mathbf{U}_{k}-\mathbf{U}_{l}\|_{H_{x}^{s-1}}\leq C_{k,l}\left(\|d\mathbf{U}_{k},d\mathbf{U}_{l}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\mathbf{U}_{k}-\mathbf{U}_{l}\|_{H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\mathbf{U}_{k}-\mathbf{U}_{l}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\nabla\mathbf{U}_{l}\|_{H_{x}^{s-1}}\right),

where
C_{k,l}
depends on the
L^{\infty}_{x}
norm of
\mathbf{U}_{k}
and
\mathbf{U}_{l}
. By using the Strichartz estimate of
d\mathbf{v}_{k},d\rho_{k},k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, i.e. (
3.2
) in Proposition
3.1
, we infer that

\begin{split}\|(\mathbf{U}_{k}-\mathbf{U}_{l})(t,\cdot)\|_{H_{x}^{s-1}}&\lesssim\|(\mathbf{U}_{k}-\mathbf{U}_{l})(0,\cdot)\|_{H^{s-1}}\\
&\lesssim\|(\mathbf{v}_{0k}-\mathbf{v}_{0l},\rho_{0k}-\rho_{0l})\|_{H^{s-1}}.\end{split}

Thus
\{(\mathbf{v}_{k},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{k}}))\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
C([-T,T];H_{x}^{s-1})
. Let
(\mathbf{v},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}))
be the limit. Then

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}(\mathbf{v}_{k},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{k}}))=(\mathbf{v},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}))\ \mathrm{in}\ C([-T,T];H_{x}^{s-1}).

(3.5)

Since
p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{k}})=\mathrm{e}^{\gamma\rho_{k}}
, we therefore have
\rho_{k}=\frac{1}{\gamma}\ln\left\{p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{k}})\right\}
. By using (
3.5
) and Lemma
2.5
, we obtain

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\rho_{k}=\rho\ \mathrm{in}\ C([-T,T];H_{x}^{s-1}).

Due to

w_{k}=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{k}}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

and

\partial_{t}w_{k}+(\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\nabla)w_{k}=0.

we can obtain

\partial_{t}(w_{k}-w_{l})+\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\nabla(w_{k}-w_{l})=(\mathbf{v}_{l}-\mathbf{v}_{k})\cdot\nabla w_{l}.

(3.6)

Multiplying
w_{k}-w_{l}
on (
3.6
) and integrating it on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\|w_{k}-w_{l}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}&\leq C\|w_{0k}-w_{0l}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\nabla\mathbf{v}_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|w_{k}-w_{l}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}d\tau\\
&+C\int^{t}_{0}\|\mathbf{v}_{k}-\mathbf{v}_{l}\|_{L_{x}^{2}}\|w_{l}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\|w_{k}-w_{l}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}d\tau.\end{split}

(3.7)

For (
3.7
), using Gronwall’s inequality, then
\{w_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
C([-T,T];L_{x}^{2})
. We denote
w
as the limit, i.e.

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}w_{k}=w\ \mathrm{in}\ C([-T,T];L_{x}^{2}).

Since
(\mathbf{v}_{k},\rho_{k})
is uniformly bounded in
C([-T,T];H_{x}^{s})
and
(w_{k},\nabla w_{k})
uniformly bounded in
H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}\times L^{8}_{x}
, we therefore deduce
(\mathbf{v},\rho)\in C([-T,T];H_{x}^{s}),w\in C([-T,T];H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}),
and
\nabla w\in L^{8}_{x}
.

Using Proposition
3.1
again, the sequence
\{(d\mathbf{v}_{k},d\rho_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is uniformly bounded in the space
L^{4}([-T,T];C_{x}^{\delta})
. Consequently, the sequence
\{(d\mathbf{v}_{k},d\rho_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
converges to
(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)
in the space
L^{4}([-T,T];L_{x}^{\infty})
. That is,

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}(d\mathbf{v}_{k},d\rho_{k})=(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\ \ \textrm{in}\ \ L^{4}([-T,T];L^{\infty}_{x}).

(3.8)

Due to (
1.20
), we get

\begin{split}\|d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\|d\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.\end{split}

(3.9)

By applying (
1.21
) and elliptic estimates, Sobolev inequalities, for
s_{0}>\frac{7}{4}
we derive

\begin{split}\|d\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\|d(-\Delta)^{-1}(\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}\nabla w)\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{3}{4}}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\|w\|_{{H}^{s_{0}-\frac{3}{4}}}+\|\rho\|_{{H}^{s}}\\
&\lesssim\|{\rho}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}.\end{split}

As a result, we have
d\mathbf{v}_{-}\in L^{4}([-T,T];L^{\infty}_{x})
for finite
T
. This combining with (
3.8
)-(
3.9
) yields
d\mathbf{v}_{+}\in L^{4}([-T,T];L^{\infty}_{x})
.

It remains for us to prove (
1.25
) and (
1.26
). For
1\leq r\leq s+1
, using Proposition
3.1
, there exists solutions
f_{k}
satisfying

\begin{cases}&\square_{g_{k}}f_{k}=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B\\
&(f_{k},\partial_{t}f_{k})|_{t=0}=(f_{0},f_{1}).\end{cases}

(3.10)

Here the metric
g_{k}
has the same formulation as in (
1.19
) by replacing
(\mathbf{v},\rho)
to
(\mathbf{v}_{k},\rho_{k})
. Using (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), we have

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f_{k}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(3.11)

and

\|f_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}f_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(3.12)

where
a<r-\frac{3}{4}
. From (
3.12
), we obtain that there exists a subsequence such that there is a limit
f
satisfying

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}.

By use of (
3.11
), we have

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}},\quad a<r-\frac{3}{4}.

Furthermore, taking
k\rightarrow\infty
for (
3.10
), the limit
f
also satisfies

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B,\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=0}=(f_{0},f_{1}).\end{cases}

Therefore, we have finished the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
∎

We next state a result with smooth, small initial data.

3.2.
A reduction to the case of small initial data

Since the propagation speed of system (
1.22
) is finite, we therefore set
c>0
being the largest one. Let us state a result with small data:

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
,
\delta\in(0,s-\frac{7}{4})
, (
1.14
) and (
1.13
) hold. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.22
). Suppose the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},{\rho}_{0},w_{0})
be smooth, supported in
B(0,c+2)
and satisfying

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq\epsilon_{3}.\end{split}

(3.13)

Then the Cauchy problem (
1.22
) admits a smooth solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)
on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which have the following properties:

\mathrm{(1)}
energy estimates

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L_{x}^{8}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

\mathrm{(2)}
dispersive estimate for
\mathbf{v}
and
\rho

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x}}\leq\epsilon_{2},

\mathrm{(3)}
dispersive estimate for the linear equation

Let
f
satisfy
the equation (
1.24
). For each
1\leq r\leq s+1
, the Cauchy problem (
1.24
) is well-posed in
H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
. Moreover, for
a<r-\frac{3}{4}
, the following estimates

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(3.14)

and

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s-1}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}).

(3.15)

hold, where
C_{M_{0}}
is a constant depending on
s,s_{0},C_{0},c_{0},M_{0}
. The similar estimates hold if we replace
\left<\nabla\right>^{a}
by
\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d
.

In the following, we will give a proof for Proposition
3.1
based on Proposition
3.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
by using Proposition
3.2
.

We divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1. Scaling
. From (
3.1
), we can see

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq M_{0}.\end{split}

(3.16)

Take the scaling

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}(t,x)=\mathbf{v}(Tt,Tx),\quad\widetilde{\rho}(t,x)=\rho(Tt,Tx).\end{split}

Let

\widetilde{w}=\mathrm{e}^{-\widetilde{\rho}}\mathrm{curl}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}.

Due to (
3.16
), we can compute out

\begin{split}&\|\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\|\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq M_{0}T^{s-1},\\
&\|\widetilde{w}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}\leq M_{0}T^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}},\quad\|\nabla\widetilde{w}_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq M_{0}T^{\frac{3}{4}}.\end{split}

Choose sufficiently small
T
such that

\max\{M_{0}T^{\frac{3}{4}},M_{0}T^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}},M_{0}T^{s-1}\}\ll\epsilon_{3}.

Then we have

\begin{split}&\|\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\|\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\|\widetilde{w}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla\widetilde{w}_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq\epsilon_{3}.\end{split}

Definitely, the above homogeneous norm is not enough for us to apply Proposition
3.2
. Next, we use the physical localization technique to reduce the data with small in-homogeneous norm.

Step 2. Localization
. Let
\chi
be a smooth function supported in
B(0,c+2)
, and which equals
1
in
B(0,c+1)
. For any given
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define the localized initial data near
y
:

\begin{split}&\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0}=\chi(x-y)\left(\mathbf{v}_{0}(x)-\mathbf{v}_{0}(y)\right),\\
&\rho_{0}^{y}=\chi(x-y)\left(\rho_{0}(x)-\rho_{0}(y)\right).\end{split}

Using (
1.18
), we next define

w^{y}_{0}=\mathrm{e}^{-(\rho^{y}_{0}+\rho_{0}(y))}\text{curl}\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0}.

Since
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
, it is not difficult for us to verify

\|(\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0},\rho_{0}^{y})\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{0}^{y}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3},

(3.17)

and

\|\nabla w_{0}^{y}\|_{L^{8}}\lesssim\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}+\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\|\rho_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

(3.18)

Applying (
3.17
) and (
3.18
), we get

\|\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}^{y}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{0}^{y}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}^{y}\|_{L^{8}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

Step 3. Using Proposition
3.2
.
Due to Proposition
3.2
, there is a smooth solution
(\mathbf{v}^{y},\rho^{y},w^{y})
on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying the following Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}v^{i}=-\epsilon^{ia}e^{\rho}c^{2}_{s}\partial^{a}w+Q^{i},\\
&\square_{g}\rho=\mathcal{D},\\
&\mathbf{T}w=0.\\
&(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0},\rho^{y}_{0},w^{y}_{0}),\\
&(\partial_{t}\mathbf{v},\partial_{t}{\rho})|_{t=0}=(-\mathbf{v}_{0}^{y}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0}+c^{2}_{s}\nabla\rho^{y}_{0},-\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0}\cdot\nabla\rho^{y}_{0}-\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0}),\end{cases}

(3.19)

where
Q^{i}
(
i=1,2
) and
\mathcal{D}
are stated as (
1.23
). As a result,
\mathbf{v}^{y}+\mathbf{v}_{0}(y),\rho^{y}+\rho_{0}(y),w^{y}
also solves (
3.19
), where the initial data coincides with
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0})
in
B(y,c+1)
. At the same time, the following Strichartz estimate also holds:

\|d\mathbf{v}^{y},d\rho^{y}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C_{x}^{\delta}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.

(3.20)

Consider the restriction, for
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\left(\mathbf{v}^{y}+\mathbf{v}_{0}(y)\right)|_{\text{K}^{y}},\quad\left({\rho}^{y}+{\rho}_{0}(y)\right)|_{\text{K}^{y}},\quad w^{y}|_{\text{K}^{y}},

(3.21)

where
\text{K}^{y}
is defined by

\text{K}^{y}=\left\{(t,x):ct+|x-y|\leq c+1,|t|<1\right\}.

Then the restriction (
3.21
) solves (
3.19
) on
\text{K}^{y}
. By finite speed of propagation and the uniqueness of solutions of (
1.22
), if we give

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{y}(t,x)+\mathbf{v}_{0}(y),\quad(t,x)\in\text{K}^{y},

\displaystyle{\rho}(t,x)

\displaystyle={\rho}^{y}(t,x)+{\rho}_{0}(y),\quad\ (t,x)\in\text{K}^{y},

\displaystyle w(t,x)

\displaystyle=w^{y}(t,x),\quad\qquad\quad\ \ (t,x)\in\text{K}^{y},

then
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)
satisfies (
1.22
) on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By use of Theorem
2.9
, we have for
t\in[-1,1]

\begin{split}&\|(\mathbf{v},\rho)\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L_{x}^{8}}\\
=&\|(\mathbf{v}^{y},\rho^{y})\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w^{y}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w^{y}\|_{L_{x}^{8}}\\
\leq&C(\|(\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0},\rho_{0}^{y})\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{0}^{y}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}^{y}\|_{L^{8}})\cdot\exp\big\{\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v}^{y},d\rho^{y}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau\\
&+C(\|(\mathbf{v}^{y}_{0},\rho_{0}^{y})\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{0}^{y}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}^{y}\|_{L^{8}})\exp(\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{v}^{y},d\rho^{y}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau)\big\}\\
\leq&2CM_{0}\exp\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{CM_{0}}\right).\end{split}

(3.22)

Owing to (
3.20
), we get

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{t}C_{x}^{\delta}}\leq C\|d\mathbf{v}^{y},d\rho^{y}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C_{x}^{\delta}}\leq C.

(3.23)

Taking
M_{1}=\max\left\{2CM_{0}\exp\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{CM_{0}}\right),C\right\}
, then (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) hold.

It remains for us to prove (
3.3
) and (
3.4
). Let the cartesian grid
2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and a corresponding smooth partition of unity be

\textstyle{\sum}_{y\in 2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\psi(x-y)=1,

such that the function
\psi
is supported in the unit ball. Consider a function
f^{y}
being a solution of

\begin{cases}&\square_{g^{y}}f^{y}=0,\\
&(f^{y},\partial_{t}f^{y})|_{t=0}=(\psi(x-y)f_{0},\psi(x-y)f_{1}),\end{cases}

where
g^{y}
has the same formulation as in (
1.3
) with the velocity
\mathbf{v}^{y}
and
\rho^{y}
. We therefore get

g^{y}=g,\quad(t,x)\in\text{K}^{y}.

By finite speed of propagation, for
(t,x)\in\text{K}^{y}
, we deduce

f^{y}=f,\quad(t,x)\in\text{K}^{y}.

Write
f
as

f(t,x)=\textstyle{\sum_{y\in 2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}\psi(x-y)f^{y}(x,t).

Due to (
3.14
) and (
3.15
), for
a<r-\frac{3}{4}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|^{4}_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq&C{\sum_{y\in 2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}\|\psi(x-y)\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f^{y}(x,t)\|^{4}_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
\leq&C{\sum_{y\in 2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}\|\psi(x-y)(f_{0},f_{1})\|^{4}_{H^{r}\times H^{r-1}}.\\
\lesssim&\left(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}\right)^{4},\end{split}

(3.24)

and

\begin{split}&\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s-1}_{x}}\\
\leq&C{\sum_{y\in 2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}(\|\psi(x-y)f^{y}(t,x)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s-1}_{x}}+\|\psi(x-y)\partial_{t}f^{y}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s-1}_{x}})\\
\lesssim&\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}.\end{split}

(3.25)

Due to (
3.22
), (
3.23
), (
3.24
), and (
3.25
), we therefore finish the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

It remains for us to verify Proposition
3.2
.

4.
The proof of Proposition
3.2
: a bootstrap argument

In this part, we will reduce the proof of Proposition
3.2
to a bootstrap argument. To start, we introduce some necessary notations and definitions. Let
\mathbf{m}
be a standard Minkowski metric satisfying

\mathbf{m}^{00}=-1,\quad\mathbf{m}^{ij}=\delta^{ij},\quad i,j=1,2.

Taking
\mathbf{v}=0,\rho=0
in
g
, which is record
g(0)
. Then the inverse matrix of the metric
g
is

g^{-1}(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-1&0&0\\
0&c^{2}_{s}(0)&0\\
0&0&c^{2}_{s}(0)\end{array}\right),

where
c_{s}(0)=c_{s}(\rho)|_{\rho=0}
. By a linear change of coordinates which preserves
dt
, we may assume that
g^{\alpha\beta}(0)=\mathbf{m}^{\alpha\beta}
. Let
\chi
be a smooth cut-off function supported in the region
B(0,3+2c)\times[-\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}]
, which equals to
1
in the region
B(0,2+2c)\times[-1,1]
. We denote

\mathbf{g}=\chi(t,x)(g-g(0))+g(0),

(4.1)

where
g
is defined as in (
1.3
). Consider the Cauchy problem of the following wave-transport system

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}}v^{i}=-\epsilon^{ia}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}c_{s}^{2}\partial_{a}w+Q^{i},\\
\square_{\mathbf{g}}{\rho}=\mathcal{D},\\
\mathbf{T}w=0.\\
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0}),\end{cases}

(4.2)

where
Q^{i}
(
i=1,2
) and
\mathcal{D}
are defined in (
1.23
), and
\mathbf{g}
is denoted in (
4.1
).

Definition 4.1
.

(Definition for the set
\mathcal{H}
)
We denote by
\mathcal{H}
the family of smooth solutions
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)
to the system (
4.2
) for
t\in[-2,2]
, with the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},w_{0})
supported in
B(0,2+c)
, and for which

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}}\leq\epsilon_{3},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(4.3)

\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{8}_{x}}\leq 2\epsilon_{2},

(4.4)

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C_{x}^{\delta}}\leq 2\epsilon_{2}.

(4.5)

4.1.
Statement of bootstrap argument

Proposition 4.1
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
,
\delta\in(0,s-\frac{7}{4})
, (
1.14
) and (
1.13
) hold. Then there is a continuous functional
3
3

3

For the definition of
G
, see (
4.8
) below.

G:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
, satisfying
G(\mathbf{0},0)=0
, so that for each
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in\mathcal{H}
satisfying
G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
the following properties hold:

(1)

The function
\mathbf{v},\rho
, and
w
satisfies
G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\leq\epsilon_{1}
.

(2)

The following estimate holds,

\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{8}_{x}}\leq\epsilon_{2},

(4.6)

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(4.7)

(3)

For
1\leq r\leq s+1
, the problem

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B,\qquad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
&f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
&\partial_{t}f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{1}\in H_{x}^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\end{cases}

is well-posed in
H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
. Moreover, on
{[-2,2]}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for
a<r-1
, we have the Strichartz estimate

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}},

and the energy estimate

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{r-1}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}.

Next, we will prove Proposition
3.2
based on Proposition
4.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
by use of Proposition
4.1
.

Observe that the initial data (
3.13
) in Proposition
3.2
satisfies

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|w_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{L^{8}_{x}}\leq\epsilon_{3}.

We denote by
A
the subset of those
\gamma\in[0,1]
such that the equation (
4.2
) admits a smooth solution
(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})
with the initial data

\begin{split}\mathbf{v}_{\gamma}|_{t=0}=&\gamma\mathbf{v}_{0},\\
\rho_{\gamma}|_{t=0}=&\gamma\rho_{0},\\
w_{\gamma}|_{t=0}=&\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{\gamma}(0)}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}_{\gamma}(0),\end{split}

and such that
G(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma})\leq\epsilon_{1}
and (
4.6
)-(
4.7
) hold. We claim that the set
A
is not empty.

Taking
\gamma=0
, then

(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})(t,x)=(\mathbf{0},0,0),

is a smooth solution of (
4.2
) with the initial data

(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{0},0,0).

Thus,
0\in\text{A}
. If we can prove
1\in\text{A}
, the proposition
3.2
follows immediately by applying Proposition
4.1
. Next, we will prove that
\text{A}=[0,1]
. Thus, it’s suffices for us to prove that
A
is both open and closed in
[0,1]
.

We assume
\gamma\in\text{A}
. Then
(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})
is a smooth solution to (
4.2
), where

w_{\gamma}=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{\gamma}}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}_{\gamma}.

Let
\beta
be close to
\gamma
. By using the continuity of
G
, we have

G(\mathbf{v}_{\beta},\rho_{\beta})\leq 2\epsilon_{1},

Similarly, (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) also hold for
(\mathbf{v}_{\beta},\rho_{\beta},w_{\beta})
. Applying Proposition
4.1
, we derive that

G(\mathbf{v}_{\beta},\rho_{\beta})\leq\epsilon_{1}.

In a similar way, the estimates (
4.6
), (
4.7
) also hold for
(\mathbf{v}_{\beta},\rho_{\beta},w_{\beta})
. Thus,
\beta\in\text{A}
and
A
is open set.

Let
\gamma_{k}\in\text{A},k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\gamma
be a limit satisfying
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\gamma_{k}=\gamma
.
Then there exists a sequence
\{(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma_{k}},\rho_{\gamma_{k}},w_{\gamma_{k}})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
being smooth solutions to (
4.2
) and

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma_{k}},\rho_{\gamma_{k}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{\gamma_{k}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{\gamma_{k}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{x}^{8}}+\|d\mathbf{v}_{\gamma_{k}},d\rho_{\gamma_{k}}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C_{x}^{\delta}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.

Then there exists a subsequence of
\{(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma_{k}},\rho_{\gamma_{k}},w_{\gamma_{k}})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
such that
(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})
is the limi. Moreover,
(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})
satisfies

\begin{split}\|(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{x}^{8}}+\|d\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},d\rho_{\gamma}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C_{x}^{\delta}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

Similarly, we also get
G(\mathbf{v}_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma})\leq\epsilon_{1}
. This implies
\gamma\in\text{A}
. So
A
is a closed set. We therefore complete the proof of Proposition
3.2
.
∎

It still remains for us to give a definition of
G
and prove Proposition
4.1
.

4.2.
Definition of the functional

In order to define
G
, we need a new foliation for the space-time and introduce new norms. Assume
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in\mathcal{H}
. Give an extension for
\mathbf{g}
(
\mathbf{g}
is defined in (
4.1
)), which equals to the Minkowski metric for
t\in[-2,-\frac{3}{2}]
. When no confusion can arise, we still record it
\mathbf{g}
.

For each
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we consider a foliation of the slice
t=-2
by taking level sets of the function
r_{\theta}(-2,x)=\theta\cdot x+2
. Then
\theta\cdot dx-t
is a null covector field over
t=-2
, and it is co-normal to the level sets of
r_{\theta}(-2)
. Let
\Gamma_{\theta}
be the graph of a null covector field given by
dr_{\theta}
. We also define the hypersurface
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
for
r\in\mathbb{R}
as the level sets of
r_{\theta}
. Thus, the characteristic hypersurface
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
is the flowout of the set
\theta\cdot x=r-2
along the null geodesic flow in the direction
\theta
at
t=-2
.

We introduce an orthonormal sets of coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by setting
x_{\theta}=\theta\cdot x
. Let
x^{\prime}_{\theta}
be given orthonormal coordinates on the hyperplane prependicular to
\theta
, which then define coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by projection along
\theta
. Then
(t,x^{\prime}_{\theta})
induce the coordinates on
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
, and
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
is given by

\Sigma_{\theta,r}=\left\{(t,x):x_{\theta}-\phi_{\theta,r}=0\right\}

for a smooth function
\phi_{\theta,r}(t,x^{\prime}_{\theta})
.

We now introduce two norms for functions defined on
[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for
a\geq 1
,

\begin{split}&|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{a,\infty}=\sup_{-2\leq t\leq 2}\sup_{0\leq j\leq 1}\|\partial_{t}^{j}f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{a-j}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\\
&|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{a,2}=\big(\sup_{0\leq j\leq 1}\int^{2}_{-2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}f(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{a-j}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

We also denote

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{a,2,\Sigma_{\theta,r}}=|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|_{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{a,2},

where the right hand side is the norm of the restriction of
f
to
{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}
, taken over the
(t,x^{\prime}_{\theta})
variables used to parametrise
{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}
. Similarly, the notation

\|f\|_{H^{a}(\Sigma_{\theta,r})},

denotes the
H^{a}(\mathbb{R})
norm of
f
restricted to the time
t
slice of
{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}
using the
x^{\prime}_{\theta}
coordinates on
{\Sigma^{t}_{\theta,r}}
.

For
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}<s\leq 2
, we now define the functional
G
as

G(\mathbf{v},\rho)=\sup_{\theta,r}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|d\phi_{\theta,r}-dt|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4},2,{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}}.

(4.8)

4.3.
Proof of Proposition
4.1

Before our proof, let us introduce two propositions as follows.

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,s_{0}-\frac{7}{4})
. Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in\mathcal{H}
such that
G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Then we have

G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(4.9)

Furthermore, for each
t
it holds that

\|d\phi_{\theta,r}(t,\cdot)-dt\|_{C^{1,\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d{\mathbf{g}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(4.10)

Proposition 4.3
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
. Suppose that
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in\mathcal{{H}}
and
G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
.
For any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[-2,2]
, the linear, non-homogenous equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{T}\Theta+B,\qquad(t,x)\in[t_{0},2]\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
&f(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
&\partial_{t}f(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=f_{1}\in H_{x}^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\end{cases}

admites a solution
f\in C([-2,2],H_{x}^{r})\times C^{1}([-2,2],H_{x}^{r-1})
and the following estimates holds:

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r-1}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}.

(4.11)

Additionally, the following estimates hold, provided
a<r-\frac{3}{4}
,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a}f\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|\Theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}\cap L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r}}+\|B\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}}.

(4.12)

The similar estimate (
4.12
) holds if we replace
\left<\nabla\right>^{a}
by
\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d
.

Based on Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
, we are able to prove Proposition
4.1
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

By using Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
, we only need to verify (
4.6
) and (
4.7
). Due to Theorem
2.9
and (
4.3
), we have

\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{8}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

Note that
\epsilon_{3}\ll\epsilon_{2}
. It implies that the estimate (
4.6
) holds. Applying Proposition
4.3
to (
2.2
), for
\delta\in(0,s-\frac{7}{4})
, we can obtain

\|d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{s-1}}.

(4.13)

Similarly, we also have

\|d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}.

(4.14)

Using (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), the inequality (
4.13
) becomes

\|d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}+\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}.

(4.15)

The estimate (
4.15
) combines with (
4.5
)-(
4.6
) yield

\|d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

(4.16)

Due to (
2.6
) and (
4.6
), we can obtain

\|d\mathbf{v}_{-}\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}(1+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}})\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

(4.17)

Adding (
4.16
) and (
4.17
), it implies

\|d\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

(4.18)

In a similar way, using (
4.14
), and combining with (
4.5
)-(
4.6
), we get

\|d\rho\|_{L^{4}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{3}.

(4.19)

Noting
\epsilon_{3}\ll\epsilon_{2}
, and seeing (
4.16
), (
4.18
), and (
4.19
), we can obtain (
4.7
). Therefore, we have finished the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

It still remains for us to prove Proposition
4.2
and
4.3
, which will be presented in Section
5
and
6
respectively.

5.
The proof of Proposition
4.2

To prove Proposition
4.2
, the key relies on discussing the regularity of the characteristic hypersurface. We begin by introducing the following result:

Proposition 5.1
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
. Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in\mathcal{H}
so that
G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Then

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|{\mathbf{g}}^{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{m}^{\alpha\beta}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4},2,{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}}+|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\lambda({\mathbf{g}}^{\alpha\beta}-P_{<\lambda}{\mathbf{g}}^{\alpha\beta}),dP_{<\lambda}{\mathbf{g}}^{\alpha\beta},\lambda^{-1}\nabla P_{<\lambda}d{\mathbf{g}}^{\alpha\beta}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-\frac{5}{4},2,{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.1)

To prove Proposition
5.1
, including
4.2
, it suffices to restrict our attention to the case where
\theta=(0,1)
and
r=0
. We fix this choice, and suppress
\theta
and
r
in our notation. We use
(x_{2},x^{\prime})
instead of
(x_{\theta},x^{\prime}_{\theta})
. Then
\Sigma
is defined by

\Sigma=\left\{x_{2}-\phi(t,x^{\prime})=0\right\}.

Let
\Delta_{x^{\prime}}
be a Laplacian operator on
\Sigma
, and

\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}=(-\Delta_{x^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.2)

The hypothesis
G(\mathbf{v},\rho)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
implies that

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|d\phi_{\theta,r}(t,\cdot)-dt|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4},2,\Sigma}\leq 2\epsilon_{1}.

(5.3)

By using (
5.3
) and Sobolev imbedding, we have

\|d\phi(t,x^{\prime})-dt\|_{L^{4}_{t}C^{1,\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}}+\|\partial_{t}d\phi(t,x^{\prime})\|_{L^{4}_{t}C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{1},

(5.4)

where we use
s_{0}>\frac{7}{4}
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,s_{0}-\frac{7}{4})
. Noting that
(\mathbf{v},\rho,w)\in\mathcal{H}
, we can obtain

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{v}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s,\infty}+|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\rho|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s,\infty}+|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|w|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4},\infty}+\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{4}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.5)

Next, we will prove Proposition
5.1
.

5.1.
Characteristic energy estimates and the proof of Proposition
5.1

To start, let us introduce two lemmas about characteristic energy estimates, which are referred to in
[
49
]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

(
[
49
]
, Lemma 5.5)
Assume
s\in(\frac{7}{4},2]
. Let
\tilde{f}(t,x)=f(t,x^{\prime},x_{2}+\phi(t,x^{\prime}))
. Then we have

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\tilde{f}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s-\frac{1}{4},\infty}\lesssim|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s-\frac{1}{4},\infty},\quad\|d\tilde{f}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L_{x}^{\infty}}\lesssim\|df\|_{{L^{4}_{t}L_{x}^{\infty}}},\quad\|\tilde{f}\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}.

Lemma 5.3
.

(
[
49
]
, Lemma 5.4)
For
r\geq 1
, we have

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[-2,2]}\|f\|_{H^{r-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}&\lesssim|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2},\\
\sup_{t\in[-2,2]}\|f\|_{H^{r-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma^{t})}&\lesssim|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2,\Sigma}.\end{split}

If
r>\frac{3}{2}
, then

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f_{1}f_{2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2}\lesssim|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f_{2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f_{1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2}.

Similarly, if
r>1
, then

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f_{1}f_{2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2,\Sigma}\lesssim|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f_{2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2,\Sigma}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|f_{1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{r,2,\Sigma}.

Next, we prove two characteristic energy estimates for the hyperbolic system (
2.1
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Assume
\frac{7}{4}<s_{0}\leq s\leq 2
. Suppose that
\mathbf{U}=(v^{1},v^{2},p(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}))^{\mathrm{T}}
satisfies the hyperbolic symmetric system

A^{0}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}+\sum^{2}_{i=1}A^{i}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{i}\mathbf{U}=0.

(5.6)

Then

\begin{split}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{U}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4},2,\Sigma}&\lesssim\|d\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{4}}}.\end{split}

(5.7)

Proof.

By a change of coordinates
x_{2}\rightarrow x_{2}-\phi(t,x^{\prime})
and setting
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}(t,x)=U(t,x^{\prime},x_{2}+\phi(t,x^{\prime}))
, the system (
5.6
) is transformed to

A^{0}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}A^{i}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\partial_{i}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}=-\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}-\sum_{\alpha=0}^{2}A^{\alpha}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}.

For
\phi
is independent of
x_{2}
, we further get
